This is quite enormous. Likely the network is constructed with many nested series and parallel combinations, and the equivalent resistance simplifies to some rational number, possibly a nice fraction.

We need to parse it carefully. The notation: S(x, y, …) means series connection of sub-networks x, y, …; P(x, y, …) means parallel connection of all those sub-networks. So we compute equivalent resistance recursively: For series: R_eq = sum of R of each sub-network. For parallel: 1/R_eq = sum of 1/R of each sub-network, so R_eq = 1/(sum of reciprocals). Equivalent to the harmonic sum.

We are told there are 400 resistors. But we don't need to count them; we just need to evaluate.

We must be careful with parentheses and commas. The expression is one large P(...) perhaps? Actually the outer structure: Network = P( ... , ... )? Let's examine the beginning: "Network = P(S(P(S(P(S(3, 3), S(P(6, 6, 6), P(3, 3, 3)), 2), ..." So the outermost call might be P(...) with multiple arguments? Let's parse the entire expression.

Better to copy the entire network string and try to interpret it as a tree. We'll need to do it step by step. Since it's text, we can write a parser in our mind, or we can manually compute nested blocks. But due to the complexity, we might need to compute it in a systematic way. Possibly the problem is from an online challenge and the network simplifies to something like 1 ohm or 2 ohms. But we must compute exactly.

Because it's a large recursive structure, we can treat it as a symbolic expression. We can compute the equivalent resistance by recursively applying series and parallel formulas.

Now we have the token list. We can now evaluate it from the innermost outward. Let's find the innermost S or P that has only numbers as children. To do that, we can look for a '(' that is followed by a sequence of numbers and commas and then a ')', with no nested '(' inside. That is, we look for a pattern: 'S' '(' numbers (',' numbers)* ')' or 'P' '(' numbers (',' numbers)* ')'. We can scan the list and find such patterns.

Given the complexity, we can do a systematic reduction: we'll repeatedly find the first occurrence of a ')' that closes a sub-expression that has no '(' inside (i.e., the most deeply nested). We can use a stack simulation. We'll go through the tokens, and when we see a ')', we pop tokens until we see the matching '(', and then we evaluate the S or P.

Let's simulate that. We'll maintain a stack of tokens. As we read tokens, we push them onto the stack. When we see a ')', we pop tokens until we find the corresponding '('. Then we have a list of tokens that are the arguments (and the operator S or P). Then we compute the equivalent resistance, and push the result as a number token onto the stack. Then continue.

This is like evaluating a reverse polish notation but with parentheses. We'll do it manually.

We'll go through the token list from left to right, and maintain a stack. I'll simulate step by step.

I'll create a list of tokens (as above) and process them. I'll keep a stack (list) of tokens. I'll write the stack after each step.

Let's start.

I'll copy the token list in order. I'll go through each token and push it onto the stack, unless it's a ')', then we pop.

I'll do it in a systematic way, perhaps using a table. Since it's long, I'll try to do it in chunks, but I'll be careful.

I'll start:

Stack = []

Process token 0: 'P' -> push 'P'
Stack: ['P']

Token 1: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(']

Token 2: 'S' -> push 'S'
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S']

Token 3: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(']

Token 4: 'P' -> push 'P'
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P']

Token 5: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(']

Token 6: 'S' -> push 'S'
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S']

Token 7: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(']

Token 8: 'P' -> push 'P'
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P']

Token 9: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(']

Token 10: 'S' -> push 'S'
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S']

Token 11: '(' -> push '('
Stack: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(']

Token 12: 3 -> push 3
Stack: [..., 3]

Token 13: ',' -> push ','
Stack: [..., 3, ',']

Token 14: 3 -> push 3
Stack: [..., 3, ',', 3]

Token 15: ')' -> now we encounter a closing parenthesis. We need to pop until we find the matching '('. The stack top is 3, then ',', then 3, then '('? Actually we need to pop tokens and collect the arguments. We'll pop until we see the matching '(' for the most recent '(' that is not yet matched. We'll do it stepwise.

When we see ')', we start popping tokens. We pop tokens until we find the corresponding '('. The tokens we pop (excluding the '(') are the arguments and possibly commas. We need to evaluate the sub-expression. The operator (S or P) should be just before the '('? Actually in our stack, the pattern is: ... operator, '(', arguments, ')'. So when we find the '(', we look at the token before it (the operator). So we need to pop until we find the '(' that matches, then the token before that '(' is the operator. Then we evaluate.

So for this ')', we pop tokens:

We pop token 14: 3 (value)
Then pop token 13: ',' (ignore)
Then pop token 12: 3 (value)
Then pop token 11: '(' (this is the matching '('). Now we have two arguments: 3 and 3. The operator is the token before the '(', which is token 10: 'S'. So we have S(3,3). Compute S(3,3) = 3+3 = 6. So we replace the whole S(3,3) with 6. That means we pop the operator 'S' as well? Actually we need to remove the operator and the '(' and the arguments and the ')', and push the result. So we pop the operator 'S' as well (token 10). So we remove tokens 10-15 from the stack, and push the result number 6.

So after processing this ')', the stack becomes:
Stack before: ['P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 'P', '(', 'S', '(', 3, ',', 3] (then we had tokens 10-15, but we removed them). Actually let's reconstruct:

Original stack after token 11: we had pushed up to token 11: 'S', '('? Wait, I need to list the stack after each push. Let's do it more systematically.
